Escrows are optics Independent Ethvestigator Program ESF funded Estonian IT Academy research measure (project 2014-2020.4.05.19-0001)


Abstract
We provide a categorical interpretation for escrows , i.e. trading protocols in trustless environment, where the exchange between two agents is mediated by a third party where the buyer locks the money until they receive the goods they want from the seller. A simplified escrow system can be modeled as a certain kind of morphism in the category of optics on a monoidal category. When objects in the base category have monoid and comonoid structures, more involved kinds of escrows ‘with intermediaries’ can be modelled as morphisms with action-like properties.

1
Introduction

You have to be trusted by the people that you lie to

So that when they turn their backs on you,

You’ll get the chance to put the knife in.

Waters & Gilmour

In their most basic form, escrows can be described as a way to enforce mutual destruction in a trustless transactional environment.

Let us unpack this definition. In our work, a
transaction
will mean a transfer of goods, whereas a
trade
will be a mutual exchange of goods defined by transactions. For example, the transactions ‘
Aki
pays money to
Bogdan
’ and ‘
Bogdan
gives apples to
Aki
’ will define a (familiar) trade where
Aki
buys some apples from
Bogdan
.
1
1

1

Our paper employs other characters than Alice, Bob and Charlie:
Aki
the albatross,
Bogdan
the bluejay, and
Candice
the crow.

The structure of a simple trade is depicted below, with the downward-pointing arrows representing the flow of time.

send money
send goods

Figure 1:
A simple trade.

Now, an environment for a transaction is ‘standard’ if trades happen in-person and in a situation where at least some basic form of law enforcement exists. In a legally standard environment, all parties in a trade can enforce exchange on the spot: if
Aki
pays
Bogdan
, but
Bogdan
refuses to give
Aki
apples,
Aki
can go to the police, refuse to leave the shop until a refund or the goods are received, or hit
Bogdan
on the head, depending on the jurisdiction in which they are. The same argument holds for
Bogdan
, which allows keeping the basic structure of trades quite simple in most situations. In an economy of scale,
Aki
and
Bogdan
can transact over long distances by relying on a network of mutual bonds of trusts defining a banking system.

Things change radically in a trustless environment, where only part of the trade can be enforced. The most typical example is internet commerce, and in particular blockchains, for the following reasons:

•

In some cases, identities are pseudonymous or fully anonymous. Even when they are not, the parties taking part in a trade often live in different jurisdictions, making the application of law enforcement difficult or impossible.

•

In some cases, the nature of the goods being traded may make it difficult or outright impossible to enforce a trade by legal means. The obvious example is people buying illegal goods (drugs or other prohibited chemicals) or services on marketplaces such as SilkRoad
[
22
]
. Here, even in a quarrel, no parties will ask the police for help lest they be incarcerated for far bigger crimes.

•

In some cases, only part of the trade can be enforced and accounted for. This problem is particularly evident in a blockchain setting: The transaction ‘
Aki
gives some bitcoins to
Bogdan
’ is publicly verifiable on the Bitcoin blockchain, and no dispute can arise in this respect. On the contrary, there is no easy way to account for ‘
Bogdan
shipped apples to
Aki
’: we will have to rely on
Bogdan
’s word.

In such a setting, since we cannot suppose in any way that
Aki
and
Bogdan
know or trust each other, we see that a massive problem arises with trades as we usually conceive them: If
Aki
pays
Bogdan
,
Bogdan
can run away without ever shipping the goods. On the contrary, if
Bogdan
ships the goods first,
Aki
can receive them and never pay
Bogdan
back. More importantly, in a trustless paying system such as Bitcoin, there is no way for
Aki
to claim money back in case of scams.

Escrows are a way to fix this problem. In their most basic form, they work as follows:

•

To buy some goods from
Bogdan
,
Aki
puts a sum of money totalling the price of the goods bought in an
escrow system
. This method consists of a vault where money can go in but not out.
Aki
, and only
Aki
, has the power to release the money at a later stage. When doing so, the money is automatically sent to
Bogdan
;

•

Bogdan
can publicly check that the money is in the escrow vault.
Bogdan
can be sure that the only way the money can leave the escrow is by being sent to him.

•

Bogdan
ships the goods to
Aki
;

•

Upon receiving them,
Aki
can release her escrow so that
Bogdan
is paid.

This system is far from perfect, and various refinements of it have been proposed.

Still, even in this basic form, we can appreciate how escrows turn the strategy ‘running away with the money/goods’ into a non-rational choice. In refusing to ship the goods,
Bogdan
doesn’t gain any money because
Aki
will never release the escrow. Similarly, in refusing to pay
Bogdan
,
Aki
will never get her money back. So, both
Aki
and
Bogdan
have no economic incentive in not upholding their side of the trade and may do so only if motivated by irrational motives such as pure malicious intent.
2
2

2

Whoever is familiar with the blockchain ecosystem –and with life over the Internet– knows that this is still a big problem. In practice, the introduction of time windows that release the escrow to the original owner if certain conditions are met can mitigate the problem. For the sake of simplicity, we will not focus on this in this work.
The basic structure of an escrow is depicted in
Figure 2
.

lock money
money locked?
Yes
send goods
unlock
send money

Figure 2:
An escrow trade.

This paper aims to describe escrows formally, with a particular focus on their compositional nature. For this, the language of category theory is fundamental. Our main contribution is showing that escrows are examples of an
optic
and that thus they organise in a category whose composition law accounts for a tangible property of this kind of transactions.

In detail, in
section

2
we define a simplified escrow system as a certain kind of optic in a monoidal category
\mathcal{M}
(e.g., the category of sets with cartesian product); escrows can be regarded as morphisms of a category
\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{M})
, with objects are the same of
\mathcal{M}
, and where the hom-objects are
\langle X,Y\rangle=\mathsf{Optic}_{\mathcal{M}}(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}Y\\
X\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}X\\
Y\end{smallmatrix}\right))
. When
X
is a comonoid and
Y
is a monoid in
\mathcal{M}
,
\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{M})(X,Y)
is a monoid in
Set
, acting on the set of optics
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B\\
B\end{smallmatrix}\right)\to\left(\begin{smallmatrix}X\\
Y\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. Moreover, in
section

4
we will define a map

\textstyle{\lhd:\langle Y,X\rangle\times\mathsf{Optic}(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}Y\\
X\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B\\
B\end{smallmatrix}\right))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathsf{Optic}(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}Y\\
X\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}X\otimes B\\
Y\otimes B\end{smallmatrix}\right))}

having action-like properties. This has the following interpretation: the object
B
acts as an intermediary in a transaction between
X
and
Y
, modeled by an escrow in
\langle Y,X\rangle
.

1.1
Related work on optics, and conventions

Optics originated in functional programming as a compositional solution to the problem of accessing fields of nested data structures; currently, optics find application in diverse branches of applied category theory like categorical probability theory
[
19
,
20
]
the theory of open games
[
6
,
7
]
(see also the connection with lenses, particular optics:
[
9
,
8
]
; see
[
21
]
for a categorical overview and generalisation) functional programming
[
15
,
14
,
2
,
4
]
; see
[
1
]
for a string-diagrammatic calculus (which is not the one we employ here; instead, we use
[
18
]
).

The blanket assumption that we make throughout the entire paper is to work in a fixed symmetric monoidal category
\mathcal{M}
. We will use the string diagram notation for monoidal categories whenever convenient. We freely employ basic facts about monoidal categories
[
10
]
, the notion of actions of an internal monoid in a monoidal category, and elementary facts about ‘coend calculus’
[
11
]
, namely the fact that given a category
\mathcal{X}
and a functor
T:\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{X}\to\textsf{Set}
its ‘coend’
\int^{X}T(X,X)
is the coequaliser
[
3
, 2.4]

\textstyle{\coprod_{Y\to X}T(X,Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\coprod_{X\in\mathcal{X}}T(X,X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\int^{X}T(X,X)}

(1)

of a suitable pair of maps defined by the conjoint action of
T
on morphisms (see
[
11
, (1.29)]
).

Although indirectly, we work with the notion of bicategory of profunctors; the bicategory
\mathsf{Prof}
has objects the small categories
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
, 1-cells from
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{B}
, denoted
\mathcal{A}\mathop{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil\kern-2.0pt$\mapstochar$\hfil\cr\hfil$\to$\hfil\crcr}}}\mathcal{B}
, are the functors
P:\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{B}\to\textsf{Set}
, and 2-cells between
P,Q:\mathcal{A}\mathop{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil\kern-2.0pt$\mapstochar$\hfil\cr\hfil$\to$\hfil\crcr}}}\mathcal{B}
are the morphisms in the category
\textsf{Cat}(\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{B},\textsf{Set})
. The 1-cells of
\mathsf{Prof}
can be composed with a ‘matrix product’ rule

Q\odot P:\mathcal{A}\mathop{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil\kern-2.0pt$\mapstochar$\hfil\cr\hfil$\to$\hfil\crcr}}}\mathcal{C}:(A,C)\mapsto\int^{B}P(A,B)\times Q(B,C)

(2)

if
\mathcal{P}:\mathcal{A}\mathop{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil\kern-2.0pt$\mapstochar$\hfil\cr\hfil$\to$\hfil\crcr}}}\mathcal{B}
and
Q:\mathcal{B}\mathop{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil\kern-2.0pt$\mapstochar$\hfil\cr\hfil$\to$\hfil\crcr}}}\mathcal{C}
.

2
Escrows are optics

In the following, we interpret objects
A
(
Aki
),
B
(
Bogdan
),
\dots
of
\mathcal{M}
as entities of some kind, transacting in money and goods. As already discussed, we assume the interaction between
A,B\in\mathcal{M}
is always non-spiteful, in the sense that we dismiss the possibility that
A
(resp.,
B
) wants to cause damage to
B
(resp.,
A
) even at the cost of losing money (resp., goods).

Morphisms
A\to B
will be interpreted from time to time as exchanges of goods, money or information between
A
and
B
. The nature of goods and information being transacted will often be kept implicit:
A\to B
means that some information or good possessed by
A
is given to
B
.

On one side, money and goods are
resources
, and as such, they can be compounded (‘if
A
gives me an apple and
B
gives me a pear, I have an apple and a pear’). On the other hand, information is not a resource and can be copied ad libitum: I can tell everyone that I have an apple as long as I want.

To aid intuition, we highlight the difference between exchanges of resources and exchanges of information by depicting the former in green and the latter in red.

Countless categorical structures have been proposed to achieve a complete formalisation of this difference. Here, we will try to get away with as few assumptions as we can.

In this setting, a simple trade like the one in
Figure 1
can be represented as a couple of morphisms

A

B

B

A

oriented from left to right.

A morphism
A\to B
represents a money transfer from
A
to
B
(in this case, Bitcoins). Similarly, a morphism
B\to A
represents the transfer of goods from
B
to
A
.

Composing these morphisms expresses the idea of
A
turning her money into goods and
B
turning his goods into money.
An escrow, as we designed it, is given by:

A

B

A
E

B

(3)

This is interpreted as follows:

•

the morphism
Lock
takes a resource owned by
A
and transfers it to a trusted escrow
E
. This can be a third trusted party or a digital vault such as a smart contract. It also sends to
B
a notification that the resource has been deposited, so that shipment can begin.

•

The morphism
Unlock
transfers the resource from
E
to
B
upon confirmation from
A
.

•

The morphsim
Envelope
represents the physical shipment and reception of goods from
B
to
A
.

An important remark on the graphical representation of optics is now in order:

Remark 2.1
.

Our treatment of optics is based on a less sophisticated idea than the one used in functional programming, i.e. the intuition that an optic is a device that can be pictorially represented as a block

S
T
A
B

(4)

‘waiting for’ a morphism
f:A\to B
to produce a morphism
S\to T
.

Notice how the morphism
B\to A
above is depicted in red. It represents the
information
witnessing that an exchange of goods has happened.

This is compatible with a setting –such as the blockchain– where the morphism
A\to B
(exchange of money) can be accounted for and publicly verified, while the morphism
B\to A
(the goods have been shipped and received), cannot.

We see that, in this perspective, an escrow is nothing more than a way to wrap an unaccountable morphism
B\to A
inside an accountable morphism
A\to B
: In other words, we split the morphism
A\to B
in two parts, and sandwich the morphism
B\to A
in the middle. Taking things apart, we get:

A

B
A
E

B

B

A

The diagram on the left now has the familiar shape of a comb, furthering our suspects that it is indeed an optic, while the diagram on the right is just a morphism. Recall the following, which is a particular case of
[
5
, 2.1]
.

Definition 2.2

(The category of optics)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a monoidal category; define the category
\mathsf{Optic}_{\mathcal{M}}
(or
\mathsf{Optic}
for short) of optics as having

•

objects the pairs
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A\\
B\end{smallmatrix}\right)
in
\mathcal{M}
;

•

morphisms
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A\\
B\end{smallmatrix}\right)\to\left(\begin{smallmatrix}S\\
T\end{smallmatrix}\right)
defined by the coend

\int^{M\in\mathcal{M}}\mathcal{M}(S,M\otimes A)\times\mathcal{M}(M\otimes B,T).

(5)

Each such morphism is called an
optic
with domain
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A\\
B\end{smallmatrix}\right)
and codomain
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}S\\
T\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.

Remark 2.3
.

With this definition in mind, we can provide an equational description of the escrow diagram in
eq.

3
. The diagram therein should clarify the aforementioned intuition that an equivalence class in the coend in Equation (
5
) consists of a pair of morphisms
u:S\to M\otimes A
and
v:M\otimes B\to T
‘waiting for’ a morphism
f:A\to B
in order to compose into a morphism
S\to T
as

\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{M\otimes A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{M\otimes f}
\textstyle{M\otimes B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v}
\textstyle{T}

(6)

Evidently, the family of maps
\tau_{M}:\mathcal{M}(S,M\otimes A)\times\mathcal{M}(M\otimes B,T)\times\mathcal{M}(A,B)
determined by the rule
(u,v,f)\mapsto v\circ M\otimes f\circ u
is a co-wedge (cf.
[
11
, 1.1.4]
) in
M
, so that there exists a unique
\bar{\tau}:\mathsf{Optic}(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A\\
B\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}S\\
T\end{smallmatrix}\right))\to\mathcal{M}(S,T)
.

Graphical manipulation provides an efficient way to handle the coend and the equivalence classes therein.

We are now ready to introduce our main definition:

Definition 2.4
.

An
escrow
from
A
to
B
is an optic
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B\\
A\end{smallmatrix}\right)\to\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A\\
B\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.

We record another definition for further use, that of a
Tambara module
: a symmetric version of the original definition in
[
13
, §3]
is enough for our purposes; a more general notion of Tambara module for a pair left/right actegory on a monoidal base is given in
[
5
, 4.1]
.

Definition 2.5
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a (symmetric) monoidal category; a
Tambara module
consists of a profunctor
P:\mathcal{M}\mathop{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil\kern-2.0pt$\mapstochar$\hfil\cr\hfil$\to$\hfil\crcr}}}\mathcal{M}
endowed with maps

\textstyle{\alpha^{[M]}_{AB}:P(A,B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{P(M\otimes A,M\otimes B)}

(7)

satisfying suitable compatibility conditions with the associator and the unitor of the monoidal structure:

•

the diagram

\textstyle{P(A,B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{P((M\otimes N)\otimes A,M\otimes N\otimes B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\star)}
\textstyle{P(N\otimes A,N\otimes B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{P(M\otimes(N\otimes A),M\otimes(N\otimes B))}

(8)

commutes, where
(\star)
is induced by functoriality of
P
by the associator map of
\mathcal{M}
;

•

the diagram

\textstyle{P(A,B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{P(I\otimes A,I\otimes B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\star\star)}
\textstyle{P(A,B)}

(9)

commutes, if
(\star\star)
is induced by functoriality of
P
by the unitor of
\mathcal{M}
.

Remark 2.6
.

Tambara modules are the objects of a category
\mathsf{Tamb}
where the hom-set between a Tambara module
(P,\alpha)
and a Tambara module
(Q,\beta)
is the pullback of certain two equalisers (we will not need an explicit description of this hom-set; the reader is invited to consult
[
5
]
for the definition).

All this will turn out useful in the proof of our Proposition
4.8
.

3
Composing escrows

Leveraging on the notions of composition for optics described in
[
17
]
and
[
16
]
, we get that we can compose escrows as follows:

A

B
A
E_{1}

B

C
B
E_{2}

C
A

B

A
B
E_{1}
E_{2}

A

B

Although very natural, both compositions are rather uninteresting. The one on the left models the idea that
Bogdan
uses the money obtained from an escrow deal with
Aki
to open an escrow deal with a third entity
C
(
Candice
).
The one on the right composes escrows with the usual optic composition and models the fact that escrows between
A
and
B
, being either optics
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A\\
B\end{smallmatrix}\right)\to\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B\\
A\end{smallmatrix}\right)
or optics
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B\\
A\end{smallmatrix}\right)\to\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A\\
B\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, can be nested into each other (indefinitely).

What we are interested in is a notion of composition that models trades happening with an intermediary:
A
opens an escrow trade with
B
, which in turns makes an escrow trade with
C
, obtains the goods, and sends them back to
A
:

A

B

C
A
E_{1}

B

C
E_{2}

C

B

A

As one can see, there is a witness
B\to A
that unlocks the escrow that
A
makes with
B
, and a witness
C\to B
that unlocks the escrow that
B
makes with
C
. These two witnesses are composed to obtain the witness that unlocks the composite escrow. This makes intuitive sense:
B
should not be able to unlock his funds until the delivery of goods from
C
all the way to
A
is completed. On the other hand,
C
depends on
B
’s behavior for their own payment. To better understand this, imagine the following scenario:
C
successfully delivers the goods to
B
, while
B
fails to deliver to
A
. In such a setting,
C
won’t be paid even if they completed their work successfully. This is because the escrow composition defers
C
’s payment to the moment when the whole escrow sequence is completed. Moreover, we see that in such a setting
B
unlocks his own escrow before
C
, and as such we informally deem
B
’s behavior as ‘greedy’. There are other ways to compose the building blocks of these two escrows to obtain a ‘gentleman
B
’ scenario where the escrow between
B
and
C
is settled indepentently –and before– of
B
’s escrow with
A
, but we weren’t able to find a convincing categorical description of this composition, which we thus omit.

We now formalize the escrow composition highlighted in the picture above. In the following, we shorten the set
\mathsf{Optic}(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}S\\
T\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}T\\
S\end{smallmatrix}\right))
as
\langle T,S\rangle
.

Definition 3.1

(The building blocks of escrow composition)
.

For a pair
(h,k)
of elements in
\langle U,T\rangle\times\langle T,S\rangle
, we define a ‘composition’

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 42.54568pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-42.54568pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\,\rule{5.0pt}{0.4pt}\,\diamond\,\rule{5.0pt}{0.4pt}\,:\langle U,T\rangle\times\langle T,S\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 66.54568pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 66.54568pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\langle U,S\rangle}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces:(k,h)\mapsto k\diamond h

(10)

as follows:

•

first choose representative elements for
h,k
inside the equivalence classes of the two coends involved: let’s say
h\in\int^{M\in\mathcal{M}}\mathcal{M}(T,M\otimes S)\times\mathcal{M}(M\otimes T,S)
is represented by a pair
(h_{1},h_{2})
where

h_{1}:T\to M\otimes S,\quad h_{2}:M\otimes T\to S

(11)

for
M\in\mathcal{M}
, and similarly,
k\in\int^{N\in\mathcal{M}}\mathcal{M}(U,N\otimes T)\times\mathcal{M}(N\otimes U,T)
is a pair
(k_{1},k_{2})
:

k_{1}:U\to N\otimes T,\quad k_{2}:N\otimes U\to T

(12)

for
N\in\mathcal{M}
.

•

second, send these pairs to the composite arrows

\Big((N\otimes h_{1})\circ k_{1},h_{2}\circ(M\otimes k_{2})\circ(\sigma_{N,M}\otimes U)\Big)

(13)

In terms of string diagrams, this is what happens: the two pairs

(15)

where a twisting
N\otimes M\to M\otimes N
has been introduced in order for
(k\diamond h)_{1}
and
(k\diamond h)_{2}
to have the correct domains.

Remark 3.2
.

This is a well-defined correspondence, i.e. it yields the same result if we change representatives in the same equivalence classes of
h,k
: in order to see this, recall that the equivalence relation in the coend in Equation (
5
) is generated by the pair of maps

\textstyle{\displaystyle\sum_{u:M\to N}\mathcal{M}(T,M\otimes S)\times\mathcal{M}(N\otimes T,S)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{l}
\scriptstyle{r}
\textstyle{\displaystyle\sum_{A}\,\mathcal{M}(T,A\otimes S)\times\mathcal{M}(A\otimes T,S)}

(16)

acting as follows for a given
u:M\to N
:

•

l_{u}(f,g)=\big((u\otimes S)\circ f,g\big)
;

•

r_{u}(f,g)=\big(f,g\circ(u\otimes T)\big)
.

The quickest way to see that the operation defined in Equation (
15
) is compatible with this equivalence relation is through string diagram representation: writing morphisms as ‘combs’ we get that

\displaystyle l_{(u,v)}(h_{1},h_{2})\diamond(k_{1},k_{2})

\displaystyle=(h_{1},h_{2})\diamond r_{(u,v)}(k_{1},k_{2}).

Proposition 3.3
.

The rule above is associative and unital: this means that the diagrams

\textstyle{\langle V,U\rangle\times\langle U,T\rangle\times\langle T,S\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle V,U\rangle\times\diamond}
\scriptstyle{\diamond\times\langle T,S\rangle}
\textstyle{\langle V,U\rangle\times\langle U,S\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\diamond}
\textstyle{\langle V,T\rangle\times\langle T,S\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\diamond}
\textstyle{\langle V,S\rangle}

\textstyle{\langle T,S\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle T,S\rangle\times\nu_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\nu_{T}\times\langle T,S\rangle}
\textstyle{\langle T,S\rangle\times\langle S,S\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\diamond}
\textstyle{\langle T,T\rangle\times\langle T,S\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\diamond}
\textstyle{\langle T,S\rangle}

commute for a choice of ‘unit’
\nu_{X}:1\to\langle X,X\rangle
.

\lxSVG@closescope
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\right)}

(18)

Unitality: define
\nu_{S}
, as the equivalence class of the pair
(\lambda^{-1},\rho)
in the coend

\int^{M}\mathcal{M}(S,M\otimes S)\times\mathcal{M}(M\otimes S,S)

(19)

(more formally, consider the summand at
M=I
–the monoidal unit; the product of hom sets

\mathcal{M}(S,I\otimes S)\times\mathcal{M}(I\otimes S,S)

(20)

contains the element
\left(\left[\begin{smallmatrix}S\\
\downarrow\\
I\otimes S\end{smallmatrix}\right],\left[\begin{smallmatrix}I\otimes S\\
\downarrow\\
S\end{smallmatrix}\right]\right)
obtained from the unitors of the monoidal structure; now project on the quotient that defines the coend in Equation (
19
)) and use a straightforward string diagrammatic argument.
∎

4
Using agents as intermediaries

In Section
3
we described escrow composition roughly as ‘to trade with
Candice
,
Aki
opens an escrow with
Bogdan
,
Bogdan
opens an escrow with
Candice
, goods are delivered, and the escrows are separately settled’. This kind of composition has its drawbacks, and to understand them, we will have to briefly venture into the exciting (?) world of legal matters.

In our escrow composition,
B
is essentially buying the goods from
C
on behalf of
A
. Legally, the trades between
A
and
B
and
B
and
C
are independent, and
B
is considered a freelancer, for which the law entails a precise taxation regime.

Nevertheless, sometimes we would like to have
B
considered as an employee working for
C
. For example,
C
may be a producer, and
B
may be an intermediate agent that stockpiles a warehouse of goods produced by
C
, selling them on their behalf. Tax-wise, these two scenarios are very different, so we want to design an escrow procedure ‘sensitive to the taxation regime’. One possibility is the following:

A

C
A
E_{A}

C
B

C
A
E_{B}

B

C

B

A

As one can see, we have a standard escrow between
A
and
C
. Independently from this, we have another
optic
where
B
puts some funds in escrow, but this time it is
A
that can unlock it. In doing so,
B
gets back his own money. In doing so,
B

is never buying merchandise from

C
since there is no transfer of money from
B
to
C
. We interpret this as having
B
acting as an
intermediary
:
B
stakes some collateral to ensure that he will not run away with
C
’s merchandise and receives the collateral back once the trade between
A
and
C
is settled. There are a couple of striking features in this construction:

•

The collateralization of
B
’s money is not an optic
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B\\
X\end{smallmatrix}\right)\to\left(\begin{smallmatrix}X\\
B\end{smallmatrix}\right)
for some
X
, and thus not an escrow according to our definition;

•

The morphism witnessing an exchange of goods from
C
to
A
is again obtained composing the witnesses
B\to A
and
C\to B
and should be used twice in this construction to fill both combs. This situation is compatible with the interpretation that combs are to be filled with red, information-like morphisms.

•

We need to pre-compose the comb with some morphism of type
B\to A\otimes B
: intuitively,
B
must be available before the escrow deal between
A
and
C
is set up, and signal his availability to
A
;

•

We need to post-compose the comb with some morphism of type
C\otimes B\to B
: Intuitively, if
B
works for
C
, some form of settlement between the two must take place after the mediated escrow is concluded.

4.1
Information and resources

Let’s start by recalling a general fact: let
\mathcal{M}
be a monoidal category, let
A
be a comonoid and
C
be a monoid in
\mathcal{M}
. Then, the hom-set
\mathcal{M}(A,C)
becomes a monoid, whose operation is called
convolution
, and it is defined as

(f,g)\mapsto f*g:\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 6.75pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 30.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 30.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\otimes A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 65.61012pt\raise 6.1111pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f\otimes g}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 87.97217pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 87.97217pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C\otimes C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 145.91928pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 145.91928pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

(21)

where
c:A\to A\otimes A
is the comultiplication and
m:C\otimes C\to C
the multiplication map.

We can use a similar idea to prove that the set of
optics

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}C\\
A\end{smallmatrix}\right)\to\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A\\
C\end{smallmatrix}\right)
is a monoid.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
A,C
be objects of
\mathcal{M}
, let
A
be a comonoid, and
C
be a monoid. Then, the set of escrows
\langle A,C\rangle
is a monoid.

Notation 4.4
.

Let
C
be a monoid in
\mathcal{M}
and let
B
be a
C
-module; the action
a:C\otimes B\to B
is usually denoted as an infix dot,
a(x,b)=x.b
for every
x\in C
,
b\in B
.

Dually, let
B
be an
A
-comodule; the coaction
c:B\to B\otimes A
here shall be interpreted as a map sending an element
b\in B
to a pair
(b^{\prime},y)
of an element
y\in A
and an element
b^{\prime}=b^{\prime}_{y}
in a new
state
depending on
y
.

Until the end of the section, we stipulate that
B
is simultaneously a module for the monoid
C
and a comodule for the comonoid
A
.

.

There exists an action

1.

unitality
axiom: the fact that
i\rtimes o=o
for every optic
o=(o_{1},o_{2})
and for the unit escrow
i\in\langle A,C\rangle
.

2.

the
action
axiom, saying that
(k\otimes h)\rtimes o=k\rtimes(h\rtimes o)
for escrows
h,k
and an optic
o
.

Axiom
1
follows from a simple string diagram deformation, using the unit axiom of the co/monoid and co/action structures:

o_{1}
a
c
o_{2}
o_{1}
o_{2}
o_{1}
o_{2}

(33)

Similarly, axiom
2
follows from the co/associativity of the co/action and co/multiplication applied to the following equality of wire diagrams, where on the left we have
k\rtimes(h\rtimes o)
(slightly simplifying its structure to avoid cognitive overload), and on the right
(k\otimes h)\rtimes o
:

k_{1}
h_{1}
o_{1}
k_{2}
h_{2}
o_{2}

k_{1}
h_{1}
o_{1}
k_{2}
h_{2}
o_{2}

(34)

This concludes the proof.
∎

Unfortunately, one can readily notice that
theorem

4.5
, albeit interesting, does not provide the notion of composition we are looking for. Roughly speaking, the most evident problem here is that our action does not even have the right type signature:
B
is present on both sides in the inside of the resulting comb so that we do not even have a place to plug in our witnesses now, that have signature
C\to B
and
B\to A
! To define a meaningful composition representing
B
acting as an intermediary, we then need to resort to the underlying structure of Tambara modules that we described in
definition

2.5
.

Assumption 4.6
.

The blanket assumption all along the following construction remains that the object
B\in\mathcal{M}
is simultaneously a module for the monoid
C
and a comodule for the comonoid
A
. Under this assumption, we can model the situation where
B
acts as an intermediary in a transaction between
A
and
C
.

Definition 4.7

(Vermittler optics)
.

(From German,
Vermittler
= intermediary) Given
A,C
a comonoid and a monoid in
\mathcal{M}
, and
B
an object which is at the same time a
C
-module and an
A
-comodule, a
Vermittler optic
over
B
is an element of the hom-set
\mathsf{Optic}(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}C\\
A\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A\otimes B\\
C\otimes B\end{smallmatrix}\right))
.

We denote
\mathcal{V}(B;A,C)
the set of Vermittler optics over
B
, making the dependence on the comonoid
A
and the monoid
C
explicit.

Definition 4.8

(
\langle C,A\rangle
‘acts’ on Vermittler optics)
.

Given an escrow
h
in
\langle C,A\rangle
,

(35)

and an optic
f:\left(\begin{smallmatrix}C\\
A\end{smallmatrix}\right)\to\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B\\
B\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, we define a
Vermittler map

\textstyle{\lhd:\langle C,A\rangle\times\mathsf{Optic}(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}C\\
A\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B\\
B\end{smallmatrix}\right))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{V}(B;A,C)}
\textstyle{(h,f)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{h\lhd f}

(36)

between the product of the hom-set of escrows
A\to C
and the hom-set of the Vermittler optics for
B
.

Theorem 4.9
.

The map in definition
4.8
is partial, and moreover satisfies ‘action-like’ properties:

•

if
\epsilon
is the counit of the comonoid
A
, and
\eta
the unit of the monoid
C
, the equation

\epsilon\circ(k\lhd f)\circ\eta=f

(37)

holds.

•

\lhd
is compatible with the action and coaction maps in the sense that the square

\scriptstyle{k\lhd f}
\scriptstyle{A\otimes c}
\scriptstyle{C\otimes a}
\scriptstyle{(n\circ f\circ m)\otimes B}

(38)

is commutative, where
m:C\otimes C\to C
is the monoid multiplication,
n:A\to A\otimes A
the comonoid comultiplication, and
c,a
respectively the coaction and action of
A,C
on
B
.

Proof.

Recall from
[
5
, 4.13]
that the hom-set
\mathsf{Optic}(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A\\
B\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}S\\
T\end{smallmatrix}\right))
can be expressed as the end

\int_{P}\textsf{Set}(P(A,B),P(S,T))

(39)

where
P:\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{M}\to\textsf{Set}
is a profunctor, and the category of integration is that of Tambara modules
[
5
, 4.1]
. Now,

•

the fact that
P
is a Tambara module yields maps

\textstyle{\alpha_{[B]}^{AC}:P(C,A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{P(C\otimes B,A\otimes B)}

(40)

that form a wedge in
B
; post-composition with these
\alpha_{[B]}^{AC}
and the universal wedge
\pi^{\bullet}
defining Equation (
39
) yields maps

\textstyle{\langle C,A\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\int_{Q}\textsf{Set}(Q(A,C),Q(C,A))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\alpha}_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{P}}
\textstyle{\textsf{Set}(P(A,C),P(C,A))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{[B],*}^{AC}}
\textstyle{\textsf{Set}(P(A,C),P(C\otimes B,A\otimes B))}

forming a wedge in
P
. This in turn yields a unique map

\textstyle{\tilde{\alpha}:\langle C,A\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{V}(B;A,C)}

(41)

by the universal property of the end that defines
\mathcal{V}(B;A,C)
.

•

The assumption that
B
is at the same time a module for the monoid
C
and a comodule for the comonoid
A
entails the existence of an action map
a:C\otimes B\to B
and a coaction map
c:B\to A\otimes B
, which plugged into the profunctor
P
yield

\textstyle{\beta_{[B]}^{AC}=P(a,c):P(B,B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{P(C\otimes B,A\otimes B).}

(42)

In a similar fashion, post-composition with
\beta_{[B]}^{AC}
, and the universal property of the end defining domain and codomain of
\tilde{\beta}
define such a map as

\tilde{\beta}:\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 33.84619pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-33.84619pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{Optic}(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}C\\
A\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B\\
B\end{smallmatrix}\right))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 57.84619pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 57.84619pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{V}(B;A,C)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

(43)

so that we obtain a cospan of maps

\textstyle{\langle C,A\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\alpha}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{Optic}(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}C\\
A\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B\\
B\end{smallmatrix}\right))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\beta}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{V}(B;A,C),}

(44)

and we can consider its pullback
Z
(in
Set
, or inside a sufficiently cocomplete ambient category). The diagonal in the pullback is the candidate partial function

\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\langle C,A\rangle\times\mathsf{Optic}(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}C\\
A\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B\\
B\end{smallmatrix}\right))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lhd}
\textstyle{\mathcal{V}(B;A,C)}

(45)

having
Z
as its domain of definition. As such,
k\lhd f
is defined as soon as
\tilde{\alpha}(k)=\tilde{\beta}(f)
, and it equals any of these two elements.

The main intuition behind this construction is the following: the object
Z
is the maximal subobject of the product
\langle C,A\rangle\times\mathsf{Optic}(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}C\\
A\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B\\
B\end{smallmatrix}\right))
with the property that the conjoint action and coaction over
B
, and all actions given by the Tambara module structure maps
\alpha^{B}
, agree. This yields a unique partial map
\,\rule{5.0pt}{0.4pt}\,\lhd\,\rule{5.0pt}{0.4pt}\,

that defines a
partial action
of the monoid
\langle A,C\rangle
on the set of Vermittler optics over
B
.

It only remains to show that this map satisfies the commutativities of (
37
) and (
38
) when the domain of
\lhd
has been suitably defined as above; it is certainly possible, but rather unwieldy, to argue equationally, with the definition and the universal properties of
Z
and the hom-sets of optics. Instead, the following remark will give a graphical interpretation of the above partial action that makes the action axioms almost tautological. This will conclude the proof.
∎

(48)

The map
\lhd
operates on the subset of the product
\langle A,C\rangle\times\mathsf{Optic}(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A\\
C\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B\\
B\end{smallmatrix}\right))
where these two actions coincide. Intuitively, this represents the fact that the escrow mediated by
B
(represented by
\tilde{\beta}(o)
) has the same effect of a standard escrow between
A
and
C
where
B
just stays idle (represented by
\alpha(h)
), which signifies that, when the mediated escrow is concluded,
B
did not receive any goods or money from any of the two parties.

To conclude, one can readily verify the ‘action’ axioms using graphical calculus, namely the fact that representing the Vermittler optic
f
as a pair
(f_{1},f_{2})
we can follow the chain of equalities

f_{1}
f_{2}
a
c

This establishes (
37
).

5
Conclusion and future work

In this work, we defined a formalism for escrow trades based on optics. We moreover investigated various ways of chaining escrows together, which can be considered more or less convenient depending on externalities such as the tax regime the participating agents are in.

As a direction of future work, we would like to obtain an alternative formalization of the same ideas relying on new techniques such as the ones presented in
[
12
]
, that allow to model processes (such as opening an escrow vault) and passing of resources as separated interactive layers.
